"Observers cannot fire it" is `G_IsObserver()` rather than a `solid!= SOLID_NOT` guard, which also catches a *dead* player. `laserhook` selects the beam rendering, which Threewave shipped as `#if 1 //def USE_GRAPPLE_CABLE` -- a compile-time choice, and the reason it has to become a cvar is precisely that a compile-time switch cannot be reported to the botlib. **The libvar push itself belongs to the bot layer**, with the rest of the ruleset -> libvar mapping: there is no botlib to push it to yet, and a check that cannot fail is not a check.

*Adds the half that makes it reachable.* `hookon`/`hookoff` were the whole implementation and **`+hook` is a console alias no Quake II client ships**, so `bind e "+hook"` -- which is what every 1999 config and every player types -- bound a key to nothing. Gladiator's own `ClientBegin` stuffs `alias +hook hookon` and `alias -hook hookoff` at the top of `ClientBegin`, with no `cmd` prefix because an unrecognised console command is forwarded to the server, and skips a bot, which has no console. Carried verbatim and gated on the ruleset rather than on `ctf_hook`: `CTFHook_f` tests the cvar itself, so a server toggling the hook mid-map neither leaves a stale alias firing it nor needs clients to reconnect. `tourney` has done the same from the start (`OSP_hookAliases`), which is why the gap was ruleset-shaped rather than visible.

*And the sweep for the same defect found it once more, in the other ruleset.* RA2 stuffs `alias +grap grap_on` and `alias +hook grap_on` at the top of its own `ClientBeginDeathmatch`; `grap_on`/`grap_off` were carried into `ClientCommand` under `RULESET_ARENA` and the aliases were not, so the arena grapple was reachable only by typing the command by hand. Both rulesets are stuffed from one place in `ClientBegin` now. Every `stuffcmd` and `svc_stufftext` in all five donors was checked against this tree and nothing else is missing. **R-CTF-4.** `botctfteam` assigns bots to a team, and it is editable from the bot menu (v0.92).

*Implemented, and the cvar had been written and never read.* The bot layer has set the `ctfteam` userinfo key from `botctfteam` from the start; nothing in the tree consumed it, so a bot under `ctf` became a `CTF_NOTEAM` observer holding a join menu it cannot press. The reader is the Gladiator donor's `#ifdef BOT` arm at the top of `CTFStartClient`, and `CTFAssignTeam` is split into the donor's pair so its balancing body is reachable without the `DF_CTF_FORCEJOIN` gate. Two decisions are **not** the donor's, because its CTF is Threewave 1.09 and has no match system: the arm runs ahead of `ctfgame.match >= MATCH_SETUP`, and a bot joining during `MATCH_SETUP` is marked ready.

*on a team is not the same as knowing it.* A play test reported CTF bots shooting their own side, and the cause is that **`clientsettings[].skin` is the only currency the botlib has for teams** -- `BotCTFTeam()` is `strstr(skin, "ctf_r")? RED: BLUE` and `BotSameTeam()` compares the half after the `/` -- while `bl_main.c` fills that field from `Info_ValueForKey(pers.userinfo, "skin")`, which Threewave never rewrites. `CTFAssignSkin` writes the `playerskins` configstring and stops, because a client is the only thing Threewave has to convince. So every bot read its own chosen skin, `strstr` found no `ctf_r`, **every bot on the server believed it was blue**, and no two bots were ever team-mates. The donor fixes it and the merge dropped the fix: `gladq2_src/g_ctf.c` adds the userinfo write to all three arms under `#ifdef BOT` -- the same fence this requirement found around `ctfteam` -- and **moves the call after the `pers.userinfo` save**, with the note saying why. Both are carried. The libvar half is the other necessary piece: the donor sets **no** `teamplay` under ctf, and that omission is load-bearing, because `BotSameTeam` tests `teamplay` first and a whole-string compare makes `male/ctf_r` and `female/ctf_r` enemies. **R-CTF-5.** Threewave has no baseq2 `spectator` flag -- observers are `CTF_NOTEAM` players -- so spectator handling and the savegame descriptors point at the fields Threewave uses, as the mission-pack port established.

*Understated, corrected.* Threewave does not merely lack the flag: it **deletes** `client_persistant_t.spectator`, `client_respawn_t.spectator`, the `spectator` userinfo key, the spectator password, `maxspectators`, `spectator_respawn()` and `GetChaseTarget()`. Colosseum cannot point at "the fields Threewave uses" and be done, because `dm` and `sp` need baseq2's and R-CORE-6 makes the struct a union. So the *question* is named once -- `G_IsObserver()` in `p_hud.c` -- and thirteen sites ask it instead of testing a field. Two of those sites are wrong in the donor and the predicate fixes both: `Cmd_Kill_f` tests `solid == SOLID_NOT`, which is also true of a dead player, and `ClientThink` gates the attack button on `movetype!= MOVETYPE_NOCLIP`, which is the right set under `ctf` only by coincidence.

*the FIFTH answer, and it was declined twice before it was taken.* Tourney's spelling is `resp.osp_entered!= ENTERED_ENTERED` -- the donor deleted baseq2's spectator system too -- and R-191 answered two sites by hand rather than widening the predicate, on the grounds that two of its callers would then diverge from the donor. By R-193 that was six sites, four of them the chase camera's own target search, so the answer is in the predicate and the two divergent callers carry an explicit arm each: `ClientBeginDeathmatch` announces an arriving observer (the donor's is unconditional) and `p_view.c` keeps `G_SetStats` for one, because the donor has no `G_SetSpectatorStats` at all. Tourney's OBSERVER INPUT is not baseq2's either and the two must not both run: baseq2 gives a spectator one key, and the donor gives it a menu, an autocam, a chase camera with a zoom and a free-look, an in-eyes mode and a target cycle. **R-CTF-6.** Player id display is on by default and shows the team icon (the 1999 fix list).

*the id view is what carried the defect of R-62.* It needs a player's name without the skin that the `playerskins` configstring carries, so it writes one to `CS_GENERAL + playernum` and puts that **index** in a stat for the client to dereference. Both halves used the compile-time constant while the line four above used `game.csr.playerskins`, and on a server that did not negotiate the protocol extensions -- the default -- the index is out of range and the server drops on the first connect. All three sites now take the runtime remap. The requirement is unchanged; what is added is that **a configstring number in this library is `game.csr.*`, never a `CS_*` literal**, for any slot the remap moves. **R-CTF-7.** The 1999 CTF bot-model bug fixed in `ClientUserInfoChanged` (v0.95) and the CTF userinfo bug (v0.93) must not reappear; both get a regression entry.

*Both entries exist and both are honestly **partial**.* Each one's trigger is a *client* -- a bot in the first case, a userinfo change in the second -- and this harness runs a dedicated server with no clients, so the observation waits for R-VER-3's bot matrix. What is discharged is structural and reviewed: under `ctf`, `CTFAssignSkin` is the only writer of `game.csr.playerskins + n`, reached from the one call site, and `CS_GENERAL + n` is written only there. Recording them as held would be the vacuous pass `audit.py` exists to prevent.

*The v0.95 half is discharged, off the wire.* R-CTF-4 puts bots on CTF teams, so the path the 1999 bug lived on is now taken, and `tools/playtest.sh` connects a client to a server with four bots on it and reads each bot's `playerskins` configstring back: all four are `ctf_r` or `ctf_b`. That is the observation the entry has been waiting for -- not that `CTFAssignSkin` is the only writer, which was already reviewed, but that what arrives at a client is the team's skin. The v0.93 userinfo half is still structural: it needs a drive script that changes `skin` mid-game.

**R-CTF-8.** `botfill` -- under `ctf` the bot count may follow the map and its two bases instead of the server. Default **0**, which is off: `minimumplayers` is the target exactly as it has always been.

*One bare `botfill` for all three rulesets* -- R-OSP-11 for why the per-ruleset naming rule never covered these three, section 7 rule 6 for why one concept keeps one implementation. One switch, and the target still computed per ruleset.

R-RA-7's argument, one ruleset over. A flat count is one number for a whole rotation, and **Threewave declares no capacity anywhere**: there is no `team_maxplayers` here and never was, `matchlock` locks a match rather than sizing one, and `warn_unbalanced` only warns. So the map is the only signal -- and unlike deathmatch there are **three pools**, because `SelectCTFSpawnPoint` sends a client to its own base while `resp.ctf_state` is 0 and to `info_player_deathmatch` for every spawn after that. A side therefore seats the smaller of **its base** and **half the shared pool**, and the target is twice that, because a capture game with uneven sides is not the game.

The pools are `G_SpawnPointPool()`'s, not the raw counts: both random selectors in this tree refuse the two spots nearest a player and choose among the rest, so a map's usable pool is two short of what it carries. Measured across the eight shipped maps -- `2 * min(shared/2, base1, base2)`:

  | map | shared | bases | target |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `q2ctf1` | 17 | 12, 14 | **16** |
  | `q2ctf2` | 13 | 8, 7 | **12** |
  | `q2ctf3` | 16 | 7, 7 | **14** |
  | `q2ctf4` | 5 | 10, 8 | **4** |
  | `q2ctf5` | 20 | 12, 12 | **20** |
  | `q2ctf6` | 14 | 10, 11 | **14** |
  | `q2ctf7` | 30 | 7, 7 | **14** |
  | `q2ctf8` | 36 | 8, 8 | **16** |

Both halves of the `min` earn their place: four maps are bounded by the shared pool they respawn into, three by a base that cannot seat the side at the whistle, and `q2ctf4` by a shared pool of five on a map whose bases would claim 8v8.

**Taking the base pools alone would be wrong**, and it is recorded because it is the obvious thing to try -- the same trap R-RA-7 records for a non-pickup arena. `q2ctf1` carries 14 and 16 team spawn points and is played 8v8; Threewave's mappers used them for variety, not capacity.

One consequence the rule owns rather than leaves to be found. **The bot a fill removes is named**: `removebot` with no name takes the lowest client slot, so a server filled to an even target could still be shrunk 4v4 -> 4v3 -> 4v2 as people arrive, and an even target is the whole point. `CTFBotFillName()` takes one off the **larger** side and returns NULL when the sides are level, which falls through to the existing any-bot call. `RA_ArenaBotName` is the same function for the same reason one ruleset over, asked per **team** -- a pickup arena's two sides are its two teams, and a real arena map's teams have no `side` until sidepick -- and it breaks a tie by team slot instead of by NULL, because under `arena` the any-bot call it would fall through to is the whole SERVER and would take a bot out of another arena's running round. Measured before it was: a `ra2map18` -> `ra2map2` level change drains seven bots out of a pickup arena (14 seats to 8) and took four off one side and three off the other, which with a person on the lighter side is 3v5 at the correct total of 8 -- and permanent, because an arena AT its target is never selected again.

A map with no team spawns at all -- a deathmatch map booted under `ctf` -- is handled rather than special-cased: an empty base bounds nothing, which is what `SelectCTFSpawnPoint`'s own fallback to the shared pool already means. The ceilings are `game.maxclients` (latched, 4 by default) and the bot list in `botcfg/bots.cfg`, and `sv ruleset` prints `want=`, `seats=`, `shared=` and `base=` for R-VER-19's reason.

**R-CTF-9.** *A CTF level ends whether or not anybody presses a key.* Threewave inherits baseq2's intermission rule -- five seconds, then the first `BUTTON_ANY` -- and inherits its hole with it: a bot never sends that bit (`bl_main.c` synthesises only `BUTTON_ATTACK` and `BUTTON_USE`), `ctf_CheckRules` returns early on `level.intermission_framenum` like every other `CheckRules` row, and `BeginIntermission`'s empty-server arm is decided once at the level end, so a client who quits on the scoreboard is not in it. A bot-filled `ctf` server therefore reached its `timelimit`, drew the board, and stopped rotating -- the same failure measured on the live `arena` server at 23.8 frozen hours out of 26.9 (R-RA-10), reached by a different ruleset. `ctf` takes the LAZY timer, `nextlevel_default`, and **not** the press delay: the defect was never how long a press is ignored for, and lengthening `ctf`'s five seconds to tourney's fifteen would fix nothing while making a player wait three times as long to leave a board they have finished reading. The match system is unaffected -- `CTFNextMap()` still owns a `MATCH_POST` level end and returns it to `MATCH_SETUP` on the same map; what changes is only that it gets there without somebody clicking. `scenarios/nextlevel` phase C is the check, and it fails on the library without this. **R-CTF-10.** *`CTFCheckHurtCarrier` is called under `ctf` only, and the gate is this project's decision rather than the donor's.* Threewave calls it unconditionally from `T_Damage` because its library only ever ran CTF; this one runs seven rulesets from one binary, so "unconditional" stopped meaning what it meant in the donor. The call is a no-op outside `ctf` and provably so rather than by inspection: it writes `resp.ctf_lasthurtcarrier`, whose only reader is `CTFFragBonuses`, which returns at `CTFOtherTeam(targ->client->resp.ctf_team) < 0` before reaching it unless the victim is on a real CTF team. So the gate changes no behaviour, and it is recorded here because **it is a judgement and not a correction** -- the rule in `CLAUDE.md` is that a shared file carrying no latch is a decision, and adding one goes beyond what the merge decided. What the gate buys is surface: it was one of the two sites that kept a NULL `attacker` live on every ruleset (R-SEC-10 is the other and is the actual crash fix), and an ungated donor entry point in a merged `T_Damage` is the shape that turned a CTF defect into a xatrix deathmatch segfault. Gating it is **not** a substitute for R-SEC-10 and must not be read as one: `M_ReactToDamage()` is id's own, is unconditional, and no ruleset gate can reach it.

### 6.5 R-RA -- Rocket Arena 2

**R-RA-1.** RA2 v2.25 behaviour from `rocketarena2`: up to 32 arenas, 256 teams, per-arena settings, the full voting set, locking, competition mode, score-by-damage, `maploop` rotation, the RA2 menus and the round log. **R-RA-1a.** The GameSpy `gstats` SDK does **not** come back, and this too is inherited (section 0 rule 6): `rocketarena2@aead6b5` removed `gstats.c`, `gbucket.c/.h`, `darray.c/.h`, `hashtable.c/.h`, `md5c.c`/`md5.h` and `nonport.c/.h`, and with them `ValidatePlayer()`, which read a `pid`/`pass` pair out of client userinfo, and the per-round `NewGame()` that resolved a dead hostname and grew an on-disk retry cache nothing drained. `ra2stats.c/.h` writes the same counters as one JSON object per round to `ra2stats.jsonl`, under `statsfile`/`statsname`. `gslog.c` is untouched and is not GameSpy code despite the prefix. Colosseum keeps this state, and there is no socket anywhere in the tree: R-SEC-7 took `netlog`'s forwarding out, `tools/noexec.py` fails the build on a socket, and `rocketarena2@b4487fa` makes the same removal upstream. **R-RA-2.** All 47 RA2 cvars and all 39 RA2 client commands work. **R-RA-2a.** *New, and it amends a default rather than adding a key.* `arena.cfg`'s `weapons:` names the nine baseq2 weapons by the digit that selects them (`2`.`9`, `0`) and the mission packs' six by word (`ripper`, `phalanx`, `etfrifle`, `proxlauncher`, `plasmabeam`, `chainfist`). **The two halves have separate defaults and separate inheritance**, because they have separate histories: the digits span exactly the nine weapons the key was invented for, so a line written before the packs existed says everything there was to say about the baseq2 half and nothing about the pack half.

  * the baseq2 half defaults to every weapon but the BFG -- `0xff`, the donor's;
  * the pack half defaults to every weapon of a content layer that is **on**;
  * a `weapons:` line owns the pack half only if it names a pack token, or `nopack`, which is how an arena refuses all six out loud;
  * a line that names none of them inherits the pack half from the enclosing block, and ultimately from that default.

**What an arena STORES and what it GRANTS are two values.** A bit whose content layer is off is carried, not cleared -- an `arena.cfg` is written once and served by servers running the Reckoning, Ground Zero, both or neither, and switching a layer back on must restore the arena as written -- so `RA_ArenaGrantsMask()` is the honoured mask and `give_ammo()` reads that. `sv arenadump` prints both numbers side by side, which is what makes the difference reviewable without a probe.

**The five pack ammunitions are each gated on their own layer**, and two of them have to be: `ammo_tesla` and `ammo_trap` are the weapon as well as the ammunition -- which is why neither has a `weapons:` bit, the donor treating `ammo_grenades` the same way -- so an ungated count is not a number on a HUD, it is an item in the weapon cycle. Gated by zeroing rather than skipping, because `give_ammo()` assigns and the inventory carries across a round.

*Why this is a clarification and not new scope.* R-182 gave the pack half no default and reasoned that a cfg naming none of the six leaves the key meaning what it always meant. The premise is true and the conclusion does not follow: 137 of the shipped `arena.cfg`'s 171 arena blocks name a `weapons:` line of their own and the other 34 inherit the default, so between them the file covered every arena and neither path could grant a pack weapon. `xatrix 1` drew two menu rows reading NO and changed nothing else. Measured on `ra2map11` arena 1 with the shipped file, `sv arenadump`:

  | layers | mask | grants | pack | ammo |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | neither | `0x00ff` | `0x00ff` | none | all five 0 |
  | `xatrix 1` | `0x06ff` | `0x06ff` | ripper phalanx | magslug 50, trap 5 |
  | `rogue 1` | `0x78ff` | `0x78ff` | the four | flechettes 200, prox 50, tesla 50 |
  | both | `0x7eff` | `0x7eff` | all six | all five |

The first row is the report that opened this: Teslas and Traps handed out by a server running neither pack. **R-RA-3.** The arena state machine keeps its seven states (`WARMUP`, `COUNTDOWN`, `FIGHTING`, `ROUNDEND`, `INTERMISSION`, `RESULTS`, `NEXTROUND`). **R-RA-4.** The published arena fixes are all present, each as its own regression entry: arena menu on connect and on `inven`; scoreboard scoped to the current arena; no more than two teams per arena; no over-filling an arena; 5-second countdown after population; `ra_fastswitch`; no starting a match in an arena that has one; winners kept between matches and spawned into the next when bot-cycling; auto-start idling below two teams; auto-start when 2+ teams observe and nobody plays; `noitems 1` default; players into arena 1 as observers on DM maps; observers ignored for spawn points; telefragging during countdown; new bots initialised into the selected arena's queue; observers ignored against `minimumplayers`; arena menu and bot assignment survive a level change.

*Makes the first two of those observable and finds them broken.* "Players into arena 1 as observers" is a claim about `pm_type`, and the only thing that can check it is a client: `PutClientInServer` must end its arena branch the way the donor does -- `gi.linkentity`, `ChangeWeapon`, `move_to_arena(ent, resp.context, 1)` -- because `init_player()` sets `fightstate` and opens the menu but never touches `movetype` or `solid`, and the free-flying body comes from `SetObserverMode()` inside `move_to_arena`. Without it `G_IsObserver()` said *observer* and the wire said `PM_NORMAL`, which no static check can see because the game was internally consistent. "Arena menu on connect and on `inven`" needs the menu to survive being closed; see R-MENU-3.

*Lands the three bot rows -- 8's cycling half, 15 and 16 -- and finds that **row 1 has been structurally false from the start**.* "Arena menu on connect" is `init_player()`, and `init_player()` was never running: RA2's `InitClientResp` sets `teamnum = -1` after its own memset, the merge kept the base, and `PutClientInServer` branches on `>= 0` -- so every connecting client took `reinit_player()` and claimed to be on whatever team is in slot 0. Row 15 ("new bots initialised into the selected arena's queue") is `RA_BotJoinArena`, row 16 ("observers ignored against `minimumplayers`") is a new arena arm in `BotCountsAsPlayer` -- under arena "playing" is being on a team, because `G_IsObserver()` there is true of the whole waiting queue -- and row 8's bot-cycling half is `ra_botcycle` (R-RA-5). Row 17's bot half is measured: a `gamemap` under `arena` with four bots keeps every one of them in its arena and on its team. **R-RA-5.** `ra_playercycle` and `ra_botcycle` behave as v0.93 defined. `ra_winner`/`ra_time` do **not** live in `gclient_t`.

The two fields are `gladq2_src/g_arena.c`'s, and R-ARENA-1 explicitly does not carry that file: Colosseum runs RA2's arena, whose **waiting queue holds both facts already**. `fill_arena` pops the front, so the order IS "longest waiting"; `fight_done` puts the winning team at the front with `add_to_front_queue`, so that IS "winners kept between matches". Adding two fields to restate what the queue says would be two more things to keep in step, so the requirement asks for the behaviour and not the storage.

What was missing is the switches. `ra_playercycle` (default 1) now gates the winner-to-front, so `0` rotates strictly by arrival. `ra_botcycle` (default 1) is the half RA2 cannot have because RA2 has no bots: Gladiator spells it `RA2_GetLongestWaitingHuman()`, and here it is "take the first waiting team that has a person on it, and fall back to the front when none does" -- **first side only**, which is the donor's own shape, because doing it for every side would empty the arena of bots. **R-RA-6.** Monsters do not spawn in `arena`. The classnames still exist (R-CORE-2); the ruleset suppresses them. **R-RA-7.** `botfill` -- under `arena` the bot count may follow the arena instead of the server. Default **0**, which is off: `minimumplayers` is the target exactly as it has always been, and nothing in the tree behaves differently.

*One bare `botfill` for all three rulesets.* `arena` is the one of the three that had a `cvar_t *` of its own and its own copy of the clamp; both are gone, and the shipped `configs/arena.cfg` sets `botfill 1`.

`minimumplayers` is one number for a whole server, and under every other ruleset that is the right shape -- one map, one game, one set of players. Rocket Arena runs up to 32 games at once and they are not the same size, so a flat count is a crowd in one arena and an empty room in the next. `botfill 1` asks the arena that `RA_AutoArena()` is feeding bots into -- the same function `RA_BotJoinArena` uses, so the census and the destination cannot disagree.

**The answer comes from two places because the mod keeps it in two places, and which one is authoritative is the whole of this rule.**

  * A **non-pickup** arena is `arena.cfg`'s own `playersperteam`, times the two teams a round is fought between. That number is not advisory: `AddtoArena` admits a team of *exactly* that size and `check_teams` ejects one that grows past it. 155 of RA2's 171 arenas declare 1, fifteen declare 2 and `ra2map1` arena 5 declares 3.
  * A **pickup** arena has no such number. `arena_init()` overwrites `playersperteam` with **128** for every one of them, because RA2 wants a pickup team unbounded, and all thirty of RA2's own pickup arenas leave the key unset anyway. What is left is the map: the `info_player_deathmatch` entities carrying that arena's number, which the two sides take **alternately** (`SelectRandomArenaSpawnPoint`), so the arena seats two out of every pair. This is also the stock-deathmatch case -- an idmap is one pickup arena and every spawn point is its own.

**Counting spawn points for the first kind would be wrong**, and it is recorded because it is the obvious thing to try. RA2's mappers used spawn points for variety rather than capacity: `ra2map8` arena 3 has thirteen and is declared 1v1, and only 55 of 141 non-pickup arenas have as many spawn points as `2 * playersperteam`. Filling one to its spawn count builds teams that `check_teams` then deletes.

Three consequences the rule owns rather than leaves to be found. **A bot pairs up before it starts a team**: `RA_BotJoinArena` gave every bot a team of its own, so a `playersperteam: 2` arena played 1v1 and its declared size was unreachable with bots -- a bot now joins an under-full **bot-only** team in that arena first, which is `menuAddtoTeam`'s shape, and never a person's team uninvited. That is gated on the switch too, so `botfill 0` is off in every sense and not only in the count. **The census counts bots in transit**: `CheckMinimumPlayers` runs before `G_CheckRules` and both gate on `level.framenum & 31`, so on a fill tick the bots `RA_BotFollowPeople` is about to move have not moved yet -- `RA_ArenaPlayers` and that function share one predicate so the fill cannot add replacements for bots already on their way. **Once each, and against the arena they are going to**: counted in the arena they are leaving as well, a bot in transit is a surplus where it stands on the same tick as a deficit where it is headed, and the removal arm throws out one of the bots that was already walking over. **And the seat is asked for in the SERVER's terms**: `numplayers` under `arena` is one arena's head count while a client slot belongs to the whole server, so the add arm tests `seated + queued < game.maxclients` beside it -- the guard the OSP arm has always carried, spelled for a census that is not server-wide. `BotFillTarget()`'s clamp answers a different question (how many the fill may ASK FOR, not how many the engine can seat right now), so without this a map whose other arenas hold the clients reads as short and the fill asks every 32 frames for a bot `G_SpawnClient` has no edict for -- `can't create bot, maxclients = N`, five times in one measured phase. **The clamp for an exhausted bot list lives in `arena.c`**, not in the cvar: the other rulesets write the achieved count back into `minimumplayers` so both arms settle on one number, and a switch cannot carry a count.

**And an empty server is filled, because that is the other half of what a flat count did.** `minimumplayers 4` seats four bots on a server nobody has joined yet -- it is one number for the whole server and does not ask who is on it -- while "an arena with nobody in it wants none" left `botfill 1` waiting for the first person to arrive before it added anybody. Two switches for one job, and the one that sizes itself to the game standing the server empty for the very people it exists to give opponents to. So the arena a bot is already STAGED in when it is added before anybody else has joined -- `RA_AutoArena()`'s own last answer -- has a target while there is nobody on a team anywhere. `RA_StagingArena()` is that answer for both of them, asked by the fill and by the bot's own join, because a target the bot is not then sent to is a fill that never converges: the arena the bots are in if there are any, else the lowest-numbered pickup arena, else the lowest-numbered arena that will have them. The first of those three is the last person LEAVING rather than the first arriving -- the game they were playing keeps its players instead of being drained a bot per tick and rebuilt next door.

The moment anybody is on a team anywhere `RA_StagingArena()` is 0 and every arena is back to wanting nobody unless somebody is in it, so the rule above -- voting the bots out of the arena you are standing in must not move eight of them next door to play each other -- is untouched: it was never about the empty map. `sv ruleset`'s `botfill` row prints `staging` when that is the target it is reporting, because `arena 1 want=8` is the same sentence for a busy arena and for an empty one and those are different servers (R-VER-19).

The ceilings are `game.maxclients` -- latched, and 4 by default -- and the bot list in `botcfg/bots.cfg`. `sv arenadump` prints `spawns=`, `want=` and `here=` for every arena and `sv ruleset` prints the target in force, because the number is computed rather than stored and a play test has nowhere else to read it back from (R-VER-19).

**R-RA-9.** *A placement is not a resurrection.* An arena placement writes `movetype` straight at the edict -- `SetObserverMode()` inside `move_to_arena()` gives a fighter MOVETYPE_WALK, and gives a lounge observer the same on a map whose arenas own an observer area (`active` false, which is every real RA2 map) -- while `player_die()` holds the respawn back a full second for the death animation. A placement inside that second produced a client that was walking and still dead, and `ClientBeginServerFrame`'s arena arm then respawned it on the frame the timer expired, pulling it straight back out of the round it had just been put in and leaving its team in `activeteams` with nobody in the arena, which `fight_done()` reads as a wipe. `SendTeamToArena` and `RA_BotJoinArena` resolve a pending death before placing -- the call the timer would have made, made now, so the corpse is left where the client died rather than where it is being sent and `PutClientInServer` clears everything `player_die()` stamped.

*It was fatal before it was merely wrong, and that half is a decision on a shared file.* baseq2's `CopyToBodyQue` copies the player's movetype onto the corpse, and there the copy is a tautology: `respawn()` is its only caller, it skips a MOVETYPE_NOCLIP spectator, and `player_die()` has stamped MOVETYPE_TOSS on everybody else, so a *walking* client reaching that line is a state the spine cannot produce. Under `arena` it can, and `G_RunEntity()` has no case for MOVETYPE_WALK -- `SV_Physics: bad movetype 4`, `gi.error()`, ShutdownGame, and a server that answers no status query while its container still reads `Up`. The donor answers it at the same line and drops the copy: `port_ra2:p_client.c` writes `ent->movetype = MOVETYPE_TOSS`, the player and not the body, which `PutClientInServer` overwrites three lines later, so what RA2 ships is a corpse left holding whatever its slot last had -- MOVETYPE_NONE out of `InitBodyQue` for a fresh slot, MOVETYPE_BOUNCE for one whose last occupant was gibbed, which `body_die`'s `ThrowClientHead` gave it. The intent is plain and the side is a slip; spelled on the body, MOVETYPE_TOSS is **both donors at once** -- the value baseq2 always computed, and a defined one for the states only arena produces -- so the site takes it with no latch. Found live on `ra2map27`, 2026-09-18: `RA_BotFollowPeople` moved seven bots into arena 1 and the round filled on top of one that had died in the arena it came from. The abort now names the edict, its classname, `maxclients` and `body_que`, because the movetype on its own is the one fact already in the source.

*Verified by donor comparison and by the arena battery, not by a scenario of its own -- and that is a property of RA2 rather than a gap in the battery.* `ra2waitroom` (mid-round deaths into the waiting room), `ra2pickuptwins` (eight round-start fills), `ra2twins` and `ra2spawn` all pass on the changed tree, which is the placement path under test; none of them hits the window, and the reason no sibling of theirs can be written to is worth stating once rather than being rediscovered.

**The round machine cannot reach its own hold, and widening the hold does not help.** `fight_done()` settles only once the corpse has CLEARED, and `arena_think` then spends a hardcoded `3 / FRAMETIME` in `ASTATE_RESULTS` before `fill_arena` places anybody (`arena.c`) -- so the next placement is three seconds behind the end of a hold that lasts one. The obvious probe does not open the window, it moves it: a build carrying `5.0f * BASE_FRAMERATE` at `p_client.c:773`, driven against the library WITHOUT this fix, put the corpse at 0.2s, its respawn at 5.2s, the round end at 8.1s and the next placement at 9.7s -- the same four seconds of daylight, further down the clock, and one round end rather than two. **And the player-facing placements are gated shut.** `menuLeaveArena` and `menuLeaveTeamAr` refuse while `ent->takedamage` is set, which `player_die()` clears only in the gib arm -- `kill` sets health to 0, which is not below -40 -- so a corpse parked on `Leave Arena` and firing `invuse` 0, 200, 400 and 600ms into its own death is refused every time. `menuAddtoTeam` places the joiner and not the team, and `check_teams` ejects only a WAITING one. What is left is the caller with no pacing at all -- `RA_BotFollowPeople` -> `RA_BotJoinArena`, which fires on a person's arena join -- and reaching it on purpose needs a bot already inside its death animation, which is how this was found live and is not something a scenario can arrange. A check that cannot enter the window passes on a library with the bug in it, so there is none.

**R-RA-10.** *An intermission nobody is going to end.* Deathmatch leaves an intermission on one condition -- five seconds, then a `BUTTON_ANY` from a connected client -- and `arena` inherited it. A bot never sends that bit: `bl_main.c` synthesises `BUTTON_ATTACK` and `BUTTON_USE` from the brain's action flags, and `BUTTON_ANY` is the engine's "any key whatsoever", which belongs to a keyboard. So a bot-filled arena server reaches its `timelimit`, draws the scoreboard and stays on it. Measured on the live server across 26.9 hours of uptime: 3.1 hours of play against 23.8 hours frozen, and ELEVEN map loads in all of it -- one a container restart, seven within 3 to 17 seconds of somebody connecting, two within half a minute of a level ending while somebody was already on, and none at all with nobody there. The longest single stall was 6h04m and was still running when it was found. `arena` takes the second of tourney's pair instead -- `nextlevel_default`, which ends the intermission with no press at all (R-OSP-1). The first, `nextlevel_click`, is how long a press is IGNORED for and is not what this requirement found; `arena` keeps RA2's own five seconds there (R-RA-10a). Zero still switches a half off, because zero is what `map_halt` writes to both. The pair is registered in `InitGame` now rather than `OSP_gameInit`, under the rule that one registration owns a name's default -- `botfill`'s rule, and the one `statsfile`/`statsname` broke in 1.21.

*The lazy half is a FRAME's work and not a client's, and that half is new for tourney too.* Both timers lived in `ClientThink`, where a clock only runs while somebody is being thought for. An intermission entered with clients who then all leave has nobody left to run it: `BeginIntermission`'s empty-server arm is decided once, at the level end, so a client who quits on the scoreboard is not in it, and every `CheckRules` row -- `CheckDMRules`, `OSP_CheckRules`, all of them -- returns early on `level.intermission_framenum`, so tourney's own "inactive client timelimit" cannot reach it either. `G_CheckIntermissionExit()` in `G_RunFrame` is the reader; the press half stays in `ClientThink`, which is where the usercmd is. **R-RA-10a.** *The two halves have different scopes, and that is the decision.* The PRESS half -- `nextlevel_click` standing in for baseq2's five seconds -- is **the OSP four and nobody else**: four, because `G_IsOspRuleset()` is `g_active_ruleset <= RULESET_DUEL` and `dm` is tourney's RegularDM here (R-MODE-7), not baseq2's. *`arena` had it too until the delay was felt in play, and the donor settles it:* `port_ra2:p_client.c` carries baseq2's rule untouched -- five seconds, then `BUTTON_ANY` -- and it is RA2's ONLY intermission exit, with no cvar and no `arena.cfg` key anywhere near it. Fifteen was tourney's default arriving with tourney's cvar rather than a number anybody chose for an arena, and section 7 rule 1 gives the donor the decision on its own feature. **Narrowing it costs R-RA-10 nothing**, which is the point of the two halves being separate predicates: what that requirement measured was a server with nobody who will ever press, and the answer to that is the clock, which `arena` keeps. The LAZY half is **every deathmatch ruleset**, `ctf` included (R-CTF-9), because there is no ruleset for which "the level never ends again" is a defensible answer; it is a liveness floor and is spelled `deathmatch->value` rather than a list that would need extending each time a ruleset is added. `sp` takes neither: `deathmatch` is 0 there, a campaign intermission is a page the player dismisses, and no rotation is waiting behind it. `scenarios/nextlevel` is the check, and its value is the control: all three phases fail on the library without this and pass with it.

**R-RA-11.** *The fill does not take a game apart when it has nowhere to put the pieces.* Three rules compose into one failure, and all three are Colosseum's own.

  * **`RA_BotFollowPeople` moved every bot it could, in one tick.** `RA_BotArenaOpen` is a question about the ARENA, it was asked once above the loop, and the door shuts behind the first bot through it. Measured on `ra2map27`: seven bots staged in the pickup arena, one person joins arena 1 -- `playersperteam` 1, `maxteams` 2 -- and SIX follow, each creating a team of its own in a two-team arena, after which the fill disconnects five to get back to a target of two. The cap is `RA_BotFillTarget`'s own number now, so "how many does this arena want" has one answer; **which** bots go is decided by client slot, because `RA_ArenaPlayers` counts a bot in transit against the arena it is going to and the mover and the census must name the same ones or the fill drains an arena it has miscounted. `RA_BotArenaOpen` is re-asked per bot as well: a cap and a locked door are different refusals.
  * **The staging arena was the lowest-numbered one holding any bot.** That is a coin toss a visitor decides: the stray they pull after them sits in arena 1 while the game everybody else was playing is in arena 8, so when they leave the server re-forms around the stray and a ten-bot pickup game becomes a two-bot duel for the rest of the map. It is the arena holding MOST bots, ties to the lowest number -- which is the old answer wherever the counts are equal, the one-arena case included.
  * **A surplus was drained whether or not anything was short.** A drain is not a move: the removal arm disconnects the bot and the add arm creates another from the roster. So it is served from the roster while the server has a seat, and a running game is taken apart only when it has not -- which is the one case where those bots really are the only ones there are.

*What the live report looked like.* `Arena wedges into a 2-bot ppt=1 countdown when someone joins and leaves`, and the arena the bots had left printed `It was a tie!` every seventeen seconds with nobody in it to fight, which is what `fight_done()` returns for an arena with no live opposition. `scenarios/ra2botkeep` is the check -- the staging fill, a visit, and the departure, asserted on `sv arenadump`'s `want=`/`here=` pair -- and it fails on the library without this.

**R-RA-12.** *`DF_SAME_LEVEL` outranks a ruleset's own map rotation.* `dmflags` bit 5 ends a level on the map it started on, and it is answered before the rotation is consulted. That is both donors' order: `port_ra2:g_main.c`'s `EndDMLevel` tests the flag and returns on it before reaching `get_next_map()`, and `port_osp:g_main.c`'s tests it -- with `manual_map != 1`, so a map somebody typed is not overridden by a flag -- before reaching `NextMap()`.

*The order is the ROW's to keep, because the split is what separates the two tests.* Hoisting each rotation into its ruleset's `EndLevel` row is what makes two rotations unable to both run (R-OSP-9), and it puts the flag and the rotation in different functions. `RA_EndLevel` and `OSP_EndLevel` each ask the flag and SKIP their own loop on it; neither answers it, because `EndDMLevel()` opens with the same test and the same map, so one site owns the answer for all three readers. `manual_map != 1` rides with tourney's guard and has no arena counterpart -- there is no such state there.

*A `DMGame` rules row ends the level through the dispatch too.* `DBall_CheckDMRules()` is Ground Zero's per-gamerule pre-emption hook, consulted by `CheckDMRules()` ahead of `timelimit` and `fraglimit` under `ctf` and `arena` (R-MODE-3); it ends the level on `goallimit` and does so with `G_EndLevel()`, the same call the function that consults it makes. `EndDMLevel()` there would be baseq2's half by name, reaching past both rotations and past the order above -- the shape `tools/dispatch.py` question D exists for, and it carries no exemption.

**R-RA-13.** *Whether the people on a server may propose playing without bots is the SERVER's answer, not the map file's.* `allowvotingbots` (R-RA-4's voting set) is a per-arena key like the twenty beside it, and `arena.cfg` is not only the voting table -- it is the map rotation, the arena definitions and every weapon and ammo default, replaced wholesale when a rotation changes and carrying map-level and per-arena blocks that override the global one three layers down. A deployment that runs `botfill` has one question it needs a durable answer to, and leaving it in that file answers it three times over with the last writer winning. `ra_allowvotingbots` is the server cvar that settles it: default `0`, which is the file deciding and is every existing server's behaviour unchanged; non-zero and every arena offers the row whatever the file said. **It is a floor and not a second switch** -- it can only turn the vote on, never off -- because the two directions are not symmetric: "this server lets people vote the bots away" is a property of how the server is run, while "this arena does not" is a property of a map, and a cvar that could withdraw the vote would be a fourth writer in a fight that already has three. Applied in `set_config()` after all three `get_settings()` layers, which is what *independent of the file* means operationally, and applied to the parsed global rather than to one arena's copy so that every consumer of that pass reads the same answer. It is registered in `set_config()` rather than `load_config()` because the reset an emptying arena triggers (`arena.c`, "Reseting to default config") reaches the second without the first. `scenarios/ra2botvote` phase F is the check and it is the whole requirement in one arm: the file says `allowvotingbots: 0`, the cvar says 1, and the row is offered -- against phase D, the same file with no cvar, where it is withheld.

**Two checks, and they answer different halves.** `tools/dispatch.py` question E is the source-level one: in the body of any function filling `EndLevel`, `DF_SAME_LEVEL` must appear before the first `G_BeginIntermission(`, which is the call that commits to a map; a row that commits to no map of its own is not asked. Its controls delete the test from each row in turn and must catch both, and its negative control is a row with neither test nor commit. `scenarios/samelevel` is the behavioural one, four arms: `arena` and tourney, each with the flag clear and set. The controls prove the rotation is live at all -- `q2dm1` -> `q2dm3`, which is not the map's own `target_changelevel` exit and so cannot be reached by falling through -- and the subjects prove the flag outranks it. Every arm refuses q2dm2, which is the answer when nothing chose.

### 6.6 R-OSP -- OSP Tourney DM

**R-OSP-1.** Tourney v2.75 behaviour from `osp-tourney`: the match system, referee and vote system, **all four modes of `m_mode` (R-OSP-12)**, the runes, the OSP hook, the observer and camera system, hi-scores, the accuracy/stats commands, the map system and the menu engine. *Five of those behaviours are the reconstruction's answer at `osp-tourney@1895f8e`, where it is byte-identical to the 1999 Linux image:* (1) a death is logged -- Standard Log and `osp_stats` both -- and drops its weapon only while a match is live, `sync_stat > 2`, so a warmup or countdown death under `dmpro`, `tdm` or `duel` records nothing and leaves nothing on the floor, and `dm`, which is always live, is unaffected; (2) the hi-score table is the scoreboard's page whenever `resp.osp_r034` is clear -- `highscores` clears it, `score` sets it, and it is clear from connect, so a client who has never pressed `score` is shown the table -- and only the alternation between table and board is intermission's; (3) the table is kept under `dm` and `dmpro` only and needs a `timelimit` or a `fraglimit`, and anything else turns it off the donor's way, by writing 0 into `client_highscores`; that 0 persists, so a server that ran `tdm` or `duel` keeps it on the maps that follow until something sets the cvar again, which the shipped `configs/dm.cfg` does; (4) `duel`'s board skips an empty seat rather than stopping at it, so the remaining player's card is drawn after the seat-0 player has gone; (5) `OSP_clearStats` blanks the match-state and ID lines in every mode, because the FFA bar draws both. Each is stated at its site with the donor function it follows, and `scenarios/ospimage` asks each of the running game as a row that fails on a library without it -- the first through the stats log and the edict census around a warmup `kill`, with `dm` as the positive arm; the fourth through the board after a seat-0 player leaves; the fifth through the match-state slot's value on the wire at a `dm` intermission.

*connecting is not entering, and the placement has to say so.* A tourney client arrives as an OBSERVER and enters through a command, so `PutClientInServer` has **two arms** keyed on `resp.osp_entered`: a client that has not entered is placed `MOVETYPE_NOCLIP`, `SOLID_NOT`, `SVF_NOCLIENT`, with no view weapon and no KillBox. `resp.osp_r240` is the other half of the same fact -- 2 for a client that has been given a body, 0 for one that has not -- and it is what `ChangeWeapon`'s gunindex, that KillBox and the autocam's candidate test read. **No join path places anybody**: all six set `osp_entered` and clear `osp_r240`, and `ClientThink` respawns the client on the next think, freezing it (`PM_FREEZE`) until it does. One trigger serves the six, and a placement that was refused retries through the same line.

*and a placement CAN be refused, which is what the retry is for.* `G_SelectSpawnPoint` is a predicate rather than a `void` row: tourney refuses a spot with a player within 60 units of it, and `PutClientInServer` leaves the client frozen and bodiless rather than telefragging whoever is standing there. Measured on `q2dm1`'s ten spawn points with eleven clients -- **4 telefrag obituaries before, 1 frozen client and 0 telefrags after**, and the frozen one placed as soon as four left. Two exclusions the shared `PlayersRangeFromSpot` does not make are load-bearing and both are the donor's: the client being placed is not measured against itself, and an observer is not a player.

*the observer and camera system, which this requirement names and which was carried without its input or its consumer.* The chase camera has FOUR controls -- forward/back zooms it between `camera_depth` and the target's eye, strafe free-looks it in four-degree steps, ATTACK cycles chasecam -> in-eyes -> out, jump cycles the target -- and `UpdateChaseCam` is where they are spent: the two modes differ in the pitch they allow (56 against 1), in whether the camera is behind the target or twelve units in front of its eye, and in whether it is lifted 30 units off the floor. What tourney does to each client **when the level ends** is here too, and was missing whole: the demo it was recording is stopped (with a screenshot at `demo_*` 2), the end-of-match music plays from `wav_file`'s five tunes or from `match_endmusic`, a team ruleset plays the winner and the loser different ones, an entered client is shown its accuracy page, its menus are closed, and the board a player who died into the intermission is owed arrives 1.25 seconds in. `nextlevel_click` (15s) is how long a press is ignored for and `nextlevel_lazy` (45s) ends the intermission with no press at all; `numgibs` is the gib count. All of those were registered cvars and carried tables with no reader. **R-OSP-2.** All 259 tourney cvars and all 137 tourney client commands work. **R-OSP-3.** The NetGames USA subsystem does **not** come back. Events are written locally as one JSON object per line to `<statsname>`, controlled by `statsfile`, `statsname`, `stats_logchat` and `stats_logallpickups`, and the Standard Log (`sl_log_method`) keeps its own file writer in `sl_write.c`; both can run at once. No `system()` call, no cleartext password prompt to clients. **Inherited, not work** (section 0 rule 6): `osp-tourney@4f17a12` already did it -- `nglog.c`, `ngmark.c`, `q2log.c`, `global.h` and the RSA `md5c.c`/`md5.h` are gone, along with 17 cvars, the `_ngws_client_id` client command, the `ngWorldStats_password` field on `client_respawn_t`, both menu rows, the `who_paused == -3` state and the scoreboard banner; `osp_stats.c/.h` is the replacement. What is left for Colosseum is to *not undo it*, and to keep the three specific holes it closed shut: the `system()` call at every map end with two unquoted 1024-byte `sprintf` paths, the stuffcmd that asked every connecting client for its own account password in cleartext, and `q2log_clientid_cmd`'s unterminated `strncpy(dst, gi.argv(1), 16)`. This is a regression entry (R-VER-1), not a task. **R-OSP-4.** Every bug the OSP port fixed stays fixed and gets a regression entry. Named explicitly, because each is client-reachable: the referee-status grant from a long address; the `strcpy` of the client address in `ClientConnect` and `OSP_getPlayerAddr`; the SIGFPE when the last human leaves a server with bots; `OSP_specbot_vote`'s off-by-one bot index; the userinfo key-order auto-ban; the two kick paths that unicast a corrupt message (`OnBotDetection`, `OSP_speedDetect`); `OSP_teamskin_cmd`'s bot arm writing the caller's userinfo; the `FL_OSP_BOT`/`FL_BOT` mismatch with no client check; `OSP_startDemos`'s `cids[2]` overrun.

All nine arrive **fixed** -- `osp-tourney` is the ported tree and this is section 0 rule 6's inherited half -- so each entry's job is to say what would tell us it had come back. Four of them are bot-reachable and had to wait for a bot that plays: the SIGFPE is a live check in `tools/playtest.sh` (a vote left running after the last human leaves a server with bots on it, which only a client can arrange), and the other three are structural against sites the bot rows now exercise. The remaining five are structural and reviewed, and say so rather than claiming a run. **R-OSP-5.** `g_spawn.c`'s `extern int botglobals;` bug -- a local `extern` declaration that zeroed `bot_globals_t.numbots` -- must not exist in any form. `botglobals` is declared once, in `bl_main.h`, and included. **R-OSP-6.** Tourney's five extra entity spawn keys -- `botlib`, `name`, `skin`, `charfile`, `charname` -- are accepted, and live in `g_spawn.c`'s `temp_fields[]` where Q2PRO moved that table. **R-OSP-7.** Stat slot allocation is **per ruleset**, from one table, enforced by a compile-time check. Measured, the donors' claims overlap almost completely above slot 15:

  | slots | baseq2 / `dm` | CTF | RA2 | tourney |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | 0 | `STAT_HEALTH_ICON` (`shared.h`) | shared | **also `STAT_SKIN_ICON`** | shared |
  | 1-15 | `STAT_HEALTH` ... `STAT_FLASHES` (`shared.h`) | shared | shared | shared |
  | 16 | `STAT_CHASE` (`shared.h`) | shared | `STAT_COUNTDOWN` | shared |
  | 17 | `STAT_SPECTATOR` (`shared.h`) | `CTF_TEAM1_PIC` | `ARENASTATUS` | shared |
  | 18-19 | **`STAT_TIMER2_ICON`, `STAT_TIMER2`** (`p_hud.c`) | `CTF_TEAM1_CAPS`, `TEAM2_PIC` | `ROUNDINFO`, `LINEPOSITION` | -- |
  | 20-21 | -- | `TEAM2_CAPS`, `FLAG_PIC` | `CTF_ID_VIEW`, `QUEUE1` | -- |
  | 22-26 | -- | `JOINED_TEAM1_PIC` ... `CTF_TECH` | `QUEUE2`, `SHOWQUEUE`, `QUEUE1_ICON`, `QUEUE2_ICON`, **`TIMER2_ICON`** | `RUNE_RESIST` ... `RUNE_VAMPIRE` |
  | 27-30 | -- | `CTF_ID_VIEW`, `CTF_MATCH`, `ID_VIEW_COLOR`, `CTF_TEAMINFO` | **`TIMER2`** (27) | `OSP_LAYOUT1` (27), popup menus (28), **`TIMER2_ICON`** (29), **`TIMER2`** (30) |

Two rows in bold change the requirement.

*baseq2 does not stop at 17.* `q2pro/src/game/p_hud.c` defines `STAT_TIMER2_ICON 18` and `STAT_TIMER2 19` for the second powerup timer. A table showing 18-21 as free for baseq2 would be wrong, and RA2 already claims slot 0 for `STAT_SKIN_ICON` on top of `STAT_HEALTH_ICON`, so "0-15 are unclaimable" was already untrue of a shipped donor.

*The second powerup timer is the hard case.* It is a **baseq2** mechanic, written by the shared `G_SetStats` in the single merged `p_hud.c` (R-CORE-7), and each donor had to find it two free slots inside its own crowded range: baseq2 18/19, RA2 26/27 (moved off 19/20 after the slot-20 collision the checker missed), tourney 29/30. One `#define` cannot serve three rulesets, so `STAT_TIMER2_ICON`/`STAT_TIMER2` -- and any future shared mechanic in the same position -- must resolve through the ruleset's slot map, not a preprocessor constant.

The conflict is only a *runtime* one where two claimants can be active at once, and the rulesets are mutually exclusive (R-MODE-1), so CTF, RA2 and tourney may each keep their own numbering. The requirement is therefore:
  1. slots 1-15 are universal and unclaimable. Slot 0 is universal too, and RA2's `STAT_SKIN_ICON` overload is a reconciliation row, not a precedent;
  2. 16 and 17 are claimable only by a ruleset that does not use baseq2's `STAT_CHASE`/`STAT_SPECTATOR` semantics -- Threewave qualifies, since it has no baseq2 spectator flag (R-CTF-5), and RA2 and tourney qualify through their own observer systems;
  3. 18 upward is ruleset-private, one numbering per ruleset, declared in one header. *Implemented* as `src/g_stats.h`'s `STATSLOT_MAP(E)` X-macro: one row per logical stat, one column per **numbering**, the kind beside the number, `-1` for absent. The X-macro form is not decoration -- clause 3 wants one header and clause 7 wants a tool to check it, and in C those pull opposite ways; this is the one text the compiler and `tools/slotkind.py` both read. `arena` and `tourney` inherited `dm`'s column until their own bars landed in Phases 4 and 5, per R-MODE-6: a column that renumbers a slot while the bar still says 18 is worse than inheriting, and the checker would have to be told to ignore it.

     *"One column per ruleset ... including `dm`'s own, which owns 18/19" would be
     false in both halves.* A column is a numbering,
     and four rulesets now share one: `dm`, `dmpro`, `tdm` and `duel` all take
     the `osp` column, because they are one code path emitting one bar. And
     baseq2's own numbering -- the 18/19 that sentence names -- is **deleted**,
     not renamed: it was read by `RULESET_DM` alone, and `dm` uses OSP's now.
     Five columns become four: `osp`, `ctf`, `arena`, `sp`;
  4. a **shared mechanic** that needs a private slot in every ruleset declares it once per ruleset in that header and is compiled against the ruleset's map, never against a bare `#define`. The second powerup timer is the reference case and the only one known today. *Implemented:* `p_hud.c`'s `#define STAT_TIMER2_ICON 18` is gone and the one shared `G_SetStats` writes `G_SetStat(ent, SID_TIMER2_ICON, ...)`. An id the active ruleset does not have is a **silent no-op**, not a write to `stats[-1]`, which is what lets shared code stay ignorant of where -- or whether -- the mechanic landed;
  5. a **modifier** may only take a slot no ruleset it composes with uses -- and the modifiers this library has -- `teamplay`, `hook`, `runes`/`techs` and bots -- claim none;
  6. `MAX_STATS` is not exceeded. With `USE_NEW_GAME_API` there are 64 slots (`MAX_STATS_NEW`) rather than 32, so headroom exists -- but only for clients that negotiate the extension, so a ruleset must stay inside 32 for its baseline statusbar and may use 32-63 only for content it can drop (R-COMPAT-5). *Exercised:* `ctf` puts the second powerup timer at **32/33**, and "content it can drop" is made structural rather than remembered -- `G_InitStats()` deletes every slot the running configuration cannot reach, so the bar items and the writes disappear together and `sv slots` reports each dropped row by name;

     *Amended: the ceiling is two bounds, not one.* Until R-ENG-1a the
     test was `slot >= MAX_STATS_OLD && !game.csr.extended` -- a question about
     the **wire** only, which was complete while `USE_NEW_GAME_API` was fixed on
     and `MAX_STATS` was therefore always 64. It is not complete now that the API
     is a build switch, because the two are independent: `extended` can be true
     on a library whose `player_state_t` holds 32 slots. The rule is the lower of
     what the array holds and what the wire carries (`g_stats.c`'s
     `stat_ceiling()`), enforced in the one place that also sizes `used[]`;
     "may use 32-63 only for content it can drop" now means droppable for **two**
     reasons rather than one, and `ctf` loses the pair under `API=old`
     unconditionally;
  7. the check is on **kind** as well as number. A statusbar element declares what a slot holds -- `stat_string N` a configstring index, `pic N` an image index, `num W N` a plain number -- and writing the wrong kind is a type error across a boundary with no compiler on it. A name-collision test cannot see it when the bar uses a bare number, which is how RA2's slot 20 got through. `tools/slotkind.py` (R-TOOL-1) is that check and its known gap is closed before the arena work is done.

     *Closed, and the diagnosis changed.* The gap was not a missing
     special case but a **missing question**. Kind alone found RA2's slot 20 by
     luck -- its two claimants happened to disagree about kind; had both been
     `pic`, a slot with two live meanings would have passed. So the check now
     asks two questions, and the second keys on the **number**: *was the slot
     available at all?* No two ids on one slot per ruleset; nothing below 16;
     nothing on a slot the pristine bar already draws by its `shared.h` name,
     even when both agree on the number, because a slot with two owners is the
     ambiguity clause 3 exists to remove; and the emitter's op, the map's kind
     and the writer's value must all agree. On `q2pro/src/ctf` it now reports
     **17, 18 and 19** where it reported 17 -- the two it used to miss are exactly
     the two whose claimants agreed about kind. Five controls ship inside the
     tool (`--selftest`) and run in `make check`, because a control kept in a
     document cannot report that it has stopped testing anything (R-35).

     *TWO MORE questions, and between them a live defect that had never
     run rather than a precaution: is the map reached through the map at all, and
     does the thing carrying an id spell it as one?* `statslot_t` is an enum, so
     both directions convert silently. `ps.stats[SID_OSP_RUNE_HASTE]` indexes by
     the id's **ordinal in `STATSLOT_MAP`** instead of by the ruleset's slot, and
     `G_SetStat(ent, ent->item->quantity, 1)` resolves whatever logical id shares
     that ordinal -- clause 4's "compiled against the ruleset's map" evaded once by
     an access that names the map and bypasses it, and once by a value that
     reaches the map meaning something else.

     Neither existing question can see either: no kind is named, so the kind
     check cannot fire, and **the map itself is correct**, so the availability
     check cannot either. Measured on this tree, and it is the largest defect the
     project has found in its own work. The tourney runes' `quantity` still held
     the donor's literal 22 while every consumer had been renamed to
     `SID_OSP_RUNE_RESIST` (28), so the pickup set an *arena* stat that tourney
     does not map -- granting nothing -- while fourteen reads took ordinals 28..32
     where the runes sat at 22..26, two of which are tourney's own
     second-powerup-timer pair, so holding a pent read as holding the STRENGTH and
     HASTE runes. And `r_count[quantity - SID_OSP_RUNE_RESIST]` was `r_count[-6]`,
     which is the counter `OSP_checkMinRunes` terminates on -- so it never rose,
     the spawner and the checker tail-called each other, and **`g_ruleset tourney`
     with `runes 1` died at map load with `ED_Alloc: no free edicts`**. It
     survived five phases, fifteen audits, ten build configurations, a twenty-row
     boot matrix and the play-test battery, because every one of them asked the
     map and the map was right; what found it was `-Warray-bounds` at
     `API=old -O2`, where the array is narrow enough for ordinal 32 to be out of
     bounds.

     The checks are now "a `SID_` never appears inside `stats[]`" and "an item
     whose `quantity` an accessor reads must set it to a `SID_` name" -- the second
     a **join between two files**, because the first attempt ("the id argument
     must be a `SID_` literal") rejected the fixed code as loudly as the broken
     code. Seven controls now, and the pair was run against the pre-fix tree
     before landing: 19 findings, 0 after. R-132 records why no existing check
     could have found it and R-VER-27's `tools/osprunes.sh` is the behaviour claim.
**R-OSP-7a.** *The statusbar is built, not stored.* Static bar strings are why the slot problem is intractable: a literal hardcodes its slot numbers, so a ruleset that needs a slot a bar already uses has nowhere to go. CTF is the worked case, measured -- slots 17, 18 and 19 each carry **two** assigned names (`STAT_SPECTATOR`/`STAT_CTF_TEAM1_PIC`, `STAT_TIMER2_ICON`/`STAT_CTF_TEAM1_CAPS`, `STAT_TIMER2`/`STAT_CTF_TEAM2_PIC`), and there was **no free slot to move to**: CTF uses 0-30 of `MAX_STATS_OLD`'s 32, leaving only 31, and the second powerup timer needs two. Upstream resolved it by gating the writes on `!ctf->value`, matching what `SP_worldspawn` already did with the bar itself. Colosseum instead emits the bar imperatively at runtime from the active ruleset's slot map (the `sb_*` model), so slot numbers exist in exactly one place and the shared second-powerup-timer mechanic stops needing three different `#define`s (baseq2 18/19, RA2 26/27, tourney 29/30). The 64 slots of `MAX_STATS_NEW` are only available under `USE_NEW_GAME_API`, which is why the CTF squeeze exists at all, and clause 6 still applies.

*Implemented and verified.* `single_statusbar` and `dm_statusbar` are deleted from `g_spawn.c` and `ctf_statusbar` never arrives; `G_SetStatusbar()` composes. The replacement is checked rather than trusted, because it replaces working code with generated code: the emitted `dm` bar is **token-identical** to the concatenation of the two literals it replaces, compared mechanically after whitespace normalisation, and `sp` emits the universal block alone, which is what `single_statusbar` was. Two things fall out that upstream's `!ctf->value` gating could not buy: the CTF squeeze is *resolved* rather than worked around -- the pent countdown displays under `ctf` on an extended server, where before it could display nowhere -- and one deliberate departure from the donor becomes a decision rather than an accident: Threewave draws the powerup timer at `xv 246` and baseq2 at `xv 262`, which is a stale copy of id's layout rather than a CTF feature, so section 7 rule 1 gives every ruleset 262. **R-OSP-8.** Monsters do not spawn in `tourney`; same treatment as R-RA-6. **R-OSP-9.** *Map rotation is per ruleset* -- the third exemption to section 7 rule 6. `sv_maplist` for `dm` and `sp`, RA2's `maploop.c` (845 lines, which is also the `arena.cfg` block/key parser and so cannot simply be deleted) for `arena`, and OSP's `osp_maps.c` (338 lines) for `tourney`. Exactly one is live at a time, and that is a requirement rather than an observation: **RA2 currently runs two at once** -- `maploop.c` and the q2pro-inherited `sv_maplist` path it really reads at `g_main.c:369-370`. That is a bug and is fixed as part of the merge. OSP registers `sv_maplist` and never reads it, which is correct and stays.

*Resolved, by placement rather than by a check.* RA2's `maploop.c` is the arena ruleset's **`EndLevel` dispatch row**, so only one rotation is reachable at all and which one is a property of the ruleset. The donor's own q2pro port put `get_next_map()` inside `EndDMLevel`, which is the double rotation this requirement names: `sv_maplist` still ran when maploop had nothing to say, and both had already decided where to go. Falling back to `EndDMLevel()` for an empty maploop is now one choice made in one place. Tourney's `osp_maps.c` takes the same row. **R-OSP-10.** *Stats logging is per ruleset* -- the fourth exemption. There are **four** subsystems, not two: RA2's `ra2stats.c` (428 lines, one JSON object per round) and `gslog.c` (339, StdLog, server-wide and arena-blind); tourney's `osp_stats.c` (867, one JSON object per event) and the Standard Log (`stdlog.c` 263 + `sl_write.c` 250). All four ship, active per ruleset. Both replacements for the dead upload services are **inherited, not work** (section 0 rule 6): `rocketarena2@aead6b5` removed the GameSpy `gstats` SDK and `osp-tourney@4f17a12` the NetGames USA layer. *The Standard Log has one dialect and `sl_log_style` is held to it.* v2.75 documents `0 = Standard Logging v1.2 (only available option)`, `sl_LogVers` emits that version, and `sl_OpenLogFile` is where the cvar and the record are reconciled: any other value prints one line naming the legal one and is reset to it. Reset rather than refused, because the operator asked for a log; after the `sl_log_method` gate, because a server that is not logging is not told about a format it is not writing. A second dialect would be a feature and not an import, so the whole of the read is the refusal. **R-OSP-11.** *Bot cvars are per ruleset.* Under the **OSP family** -- `dm`, `dmpro`, `tdm`, `duel` -- the names are tourney's: `bots_autoload`, `bots_minplayers` (default 4, so bots are on out of the box), `bots_botfile`, `bots_delayload`, `bots_warmuptime`, `bots_noclients`, `vote_bots_max`. Under `ctf` and `arena` they are `minimumplayers` and `botfile`. Both sets are registered; `docs/cvars.md` states which is authoritative per ruleset.

*Widens the first set from one ruleset to four and narrows the second from four to two*, because `dm` is OSP's `RegularDM` now. The contract is preserved, not redesigned -- which is this rule's own closing clause -- but an operator's hand-written `dm` config that sets `minimumplayers` stops being read, and that is the one migration this rule cannot make silently. `configs/dm.cfg` carries the new name and `colosseum/README.md` says so.

**The bot-*fill* switch is the exception, and it is unified.** There is one bare `botfill` for `arena`, `ctf` and `dm` (R-RA-7, R-CTF-8, R-DM-1). It is not covered by this rule and never was: this rule governs cvars a **donor** named, so that each donor's own readme and configs keep spelling its cvars its own way. The fill switch is Colosseum's own invention, appears in no donor's documentation, and names one concept -- so section 7 rule 6 governs it instead, and it says keep one. `BotFillCvar()` is deleted. Bots under the OSP four keep the behaviour the port already implements and it is more nuanced than "parity or match-only": bots ready themselves up after `bots_warmuptime`, join teams and are counted, but are already excluded from 1v1 (`bots_autoload == 2` zeroes `bots_minplayers`), from hi-scores, from team captaincy and from vote quorum, and bot management is already gated behind `serveronlybotcmds` and `vote_enable_bots`. That is the contract; it is preserved rather than redesigned.

*And it turns out the port states the contract and does not implement the half that needs a bot to act.* A client under the OSP four connects as an **observer** and enters by pressing a key; nothing presses it for a bot, in this tree or in `osp-tourney`. The botlib cannot -- its whole client-command vocabulary is `say`, `say_team`, `use`, `drop`, `invuse`, `invdrop`, `wave` and `EA_Command` -- and neither `bl_*.c` nor `bots.cfg` issues a join. So `OSP_botJoin` calls `OSP_startObserve`, the donor's own toggle, which already handles all four modes including `OSP_1v1AllowJoin` under `duel` and `OSP_addTeamMember(ent, 2)` under `tdm` and `duel`; and `OSP_botReady` implements `bots_warmuptime` as documented, with "all other real clients have readied" read as vacuously true on a server with no humans -- which is the reading `OSP_ready_cmd`'s own "everybody left is a bot, start without waiting them out" shortcut already takes, and that shortcut was unreachable until something readied a bot. Measured across all four modes on `q2dm1`. **R-OSP-12.** *Tourney's four modes of play are four rulesets.* A latched cvar `match_mode`, cached into a global `m_mode`, selected among four structures of play **inside** the `tourney` ruleset. They are now `g_ruleset` values in their own right -- `dm`, `dmpro`, `tdm`, `duel` -- and `match_mode`, `m_mode` and the name `tourney` are gone (R-MODE-1, R-MODE-2).

What the four *are* is unchanged, and all four remain **inherited, not work** (section 0 rule 6) -- `osp-tourney@a8e30d0` implements them. Measured, with the name against each:

  | ruleset | was | `match_type` (serverinfo) | banner | structure | shipped configs |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | `dm` | `match_mode 0` | `RegularDM` | `*** REGULAR DEATHMATCH ***` | Plain FFA. No ready-up, `sync_stat` starts at 8. The only mode with the `highscores` command and with `qualifier_numspots` forced to 0; with `dmpro`, one of the two that keep the hi-score table (R-OSP-1). | 7 |
  | `dmpro` | `match_mode 1` | `QualifierDM` | `*** DM QUALIFIER ***` | FFA behind a ready gate; the top `qualifier_numspots` fraggers qualify and are starred on the scoreboard. Adds `qualifier_skinname` and `qualifier_forceskins`. | 1 |
  | `tdm` | `match_mode 2` | `TeamPlay` | `*** DM TEAM-PLAY MODE ***` | Two teams: captains, join codes, invites, team lock/unlock, `switchteam`, `kickplayer`, overtime. | 4 |
  | `duel` | `match_mode 3` | `1-vs-1` | `*** DM 1V1 MODE ***` | Duel. Two teams of one, forced. Adds a **spectator queue**: winner stays, the next in line has `team_nextuptime` seconds to claim the slot or forfeits it. Timeouts are per individual, not per team. | 3 |

The banner block was `osp_main.c:564-586`; the mod documents the same four at `docs/server-settings.txt:413-419`. The banners and `match_type` survive the flattening -- `match_type` is still published to serverinfo, derived from the ruleset now rather than from a cvar, because server browsers read it and `osp_stats.c` writes it into every JSON record.

**`duel` is a first-class ruleset, not a corner of `tdm`**, and this is the clause that mattered for the port and now for the flattening: **34** `m_mode == 3` sites across **10** files, six dedicated helpers in `osp_teams.c` (`OSP_1v1Team`:329, `OSP_1v1AllowJoin`:370, `OSP_1v1Add`:421, `OSP_1v1Remove`:435, `OSP_1v1QueueCheck`:471, `OSP_1v1queue_cmd`:1658), its own client command under three aliases -- `queue`, `line`, `order` (`g_cmds.c:1217-1219`) -- its own statusbar branch (`p_hud.c:172`), its own scoreboard branch (`osp_display.c:337`), its own observer, camera and menu join rules (`osp_observe.c` x5, `p_camera.c` x3, `osp_menus.c` x4), and four cvar overrides applied at `InitGame`: `team_maxplayers` -> 1 `CVAR_NOSET` (`osp_main.c:479-484`), its own `team_maxplayers` default of 1 rather than 4 (`osp_main.c:404-407`), `bots_minplayers` -> 0 when `bots_autoload == 2` (`osp_main.c:651-653`), and a `match_latejoin` override (`osp_main.c:2137`). A merge that treats mode 3 as "mode 2 with two players" loses every one of them, and so does a flattening that gives `duel` `tdm`'s ops row and stops there. Measured again against this tree rather than the donor: **36** `m_mode == 3` sites, in a `m_mode` surface of **234** sites across **22** files, twelve of them shared files rather than `src/tourney/`'s own. **There is no `match_mode`, and so nothing to validate.** A rule for it would have existed because the cvar that chose the mode of play was the one bounded tourney cvar the donor never range-checked, so the banner block's final `else` caught everything outside 0..2 and `match_mode 4` announced `*** DM 1V1 MODE ***`, set `match_type` to `1-vs-1`, and skipped all 34 `m_mode == 3` branches: no queue, no forced `team_maxplayers 1`, team-scoped timeouts. **The server told its clients it was a duel server and was not one**, and a negative value did the same. It was never implemented (`OSP_clampMatchMode()`), was found unimplemented by R-VER-16, and the donor reached the same finding independently in `osp-tourney@e908b62` -- the two differing only on what an out-of-range value becomes (the donor clamps 4 -> 3, this fell back to 0).

The flattening removes the class of defect rather than the check. A mode is a `g_ruleset` value now, and an unknown one takes R-MODE-1's path: warn, fall back to `dm`, never abort. There is no numeric range to sit outside of, no banner block choosing on a bare `else`, and no way for the announced mode and the running one to disagree, because they are the same enum. `match_type` is derived from the ruleset, so serverinfo cannot drift from behaviour either. What R-OSP-13 was defending is now true by construction (R-MODE-1, R-OSP-12).

**R-OSP-14.** *Tourney's scoreboard is reached through the `ScoreboardMessage` row, never by name.* OSP **deleted** baseq2's `DeathmatchScoreboardMessage` and put its own five-page dispatcher under that name (`port_osp@205a89c` `p_hud.c:140-151`): `resp.osp_r24c` picks the player card, the MOTD, the match parameters or the previous match's scores, and only then does the mode pick a board. Three of tourney's own functions select a page and then draw it -- `OSP_setStats`' ten-second MOTD window, `OSP_highscores_cmd` and `OSP_showinfo_cmd` -- so each of them is `G_ScoreboardMessage()` here, because here that dispatcher **is** the row.

*The name is the trap, and that is why the rule is written down rather than left to whoever reads the donor next.* `DeathmatchScoreboardMessage` exists in this tree and is baseq2's grid board. A call to it from tourney compiles, links, runs and puts a scoreboard on the screen; the only thing wrong with it is that OSP has no such screen, so the MOTD window would answer a connecting player with an avatar grid and `highscores` would set `resp.osp_r034` for nobody to read. Neither is visible to a compiler or to any audit that asks whether a symbol exists. This row is therefore gate-only, and an exemption for it would be a claim about a donor that the donor's own source contradicts. `tools/dispatch.py` question D is the standing check -- a by-name call to an `ops_base` implementation is legitimate only as a same-row fallback -- and the `dm` row of `tools/playtest.sh`'s client battery reads the window's page off the wire, because which screen a connecting player is shown is not a question the source can be asked.

**R-OSP-15.** *Tourney's intermission timers run without a client.* Both were read from `ClientThink` alone, so the lazy one could not fire on a server whose last client quit while the scoreboard was up -- and nothing else could end that level either, `OSP_CheckRules`' "inactive client timelimit" being unreachable during an intermission like every other `CheckRules` row. The reader is `G_CheckIntermissionExit()` in `G_RunFrame` now. The timers, their values and `map_halt`'s zeroing of them are unchanged; what changed is that something runs them. Shared with `arena` (R-RA-10), which is also why the two cvars moved to `InitGame`. `scenarios/nextlevel` phase B is the check.

**R-OSP-16.** *A vote that takes bots out takes out the bots that are there, and the fill does not put them back.* `arena` has had the players' half of this since its per-arena `bots` switch became votable: the people in an arena vote it off and `RA_BotsVotedOut()` empties the arena of bots that the fill then stops selecting it for. The OSP four had the row and could not reach a single bot with it on any server that actually runs them -- and that is two independent failures, either of which is enough on its own.

  * **The cap counted the wrong bots.** `vote rembot <n>` refused any `n` above `bots_votedin`, and the "Remove random bots" menu row stepped over the same range. That counter is the bots a VOTE put there; every bot seated by `botfill` or by a flat `bots_minplayers` is outside it, so a server holding eight of them answered *"You can remove only 0 more bots"* while the menu two rows below said *"Total active bots: 8"*. The cap is the bot census now -- `botglobals.numbots`, which both of those rows already read -- and the command's no-argument line prints both numbers rather than only the voted-in one, because after this they are different questions.
  * **The removal did not hold.** `CheckMinimumPlayers` compares the census against the target every 32 frames, so a bot taken out of a filled server is a deficit on the next tick and comes straight back -- while the passing vote is still on the screen. `bots_votedout` is the counter that closes it, tourney's own global beside `bots_votedin` and read by the bot layer through `BotTourneyVotedOut()` (R-OSP-11's accessor rule). `BotFillTarget()` subtracts it from the computed target and `CheckMinimumPlayers` subtracts it from the flat count, which is the one arm that never goes through that function. The TARGET comes down by what the vote took off, which is what makes the removal permanent without a second removal loop.

**Voted-in bots come off first, and that is arithmetic rather than courtesy.** A bot a vote added is already outside the fill's target -- that is precisely what `numplayers - bots_votedin - 1` means in `CheckMinimumPlayers` -- so removing one asks the fill for nothing, and only the remainder is a bot the FILL seated and has to be told about. So a `rembot 3` on a server with two voted-in bots zeroes that counter and cuts the target by one. The number it works from is what the removal ACTUALLY took off, counted either side of the loop rather than taken from the vote: a bot can leave between a vote being put and passing, and a cut is a subtraction nothing corrects until the level ends. **Counted, and not read off `botglobals.numbots`**, which is a cache a dozen sites write and which is wrong in exactly this window: `BotDestroy()` calls `ClientDisconnect()` before its own `numbots--`, tourney's disconnect path recounts the clients there, and the departing bot is already out of that count -- so each removal takes the cache down by two and it sits one below the truth until the next frame corrects it. A difference read from it cut the target by one bot too many per vote, which is a seat the fill would never fill again. The cache itself is left alone: it self-corrects, and a vote is not the place to change what every ruleset reads. `OSP_botCount()` is the walk, and the cap, the menu row and the removal all ask it, so a refusal and the row that provoked it cannot disagree. `vote kick` on a bot takes the same order for the same reason, because it is the other row that removes one and it had the same fault.
